Large Scale Dynamical Response of Interacting 1 d d Fermi Systems
Marcello Porta, Giuseppe Scola, Harman Preet Singh

Abstract
We consider the dynamics of a class of weakly interacting, gapless 1 ​ d 1d fermionic systems, in presence of small external perturbations slowly varying in space and in time. We consider the evolution of the expectation values of the charge density and of the current density, in the thermodynamic limit and for low enough temperatures. We prove the validity and the asymptotic exactness of linear response in the limit of vanishing space-time variation of the perturbation, and we provide the explicit expression of the response of the system. The proof relies on the representation of the real time Duhamel expansion in terms of Euclidean correlation functions, for which we provide sharp estimates using rigorous renormalization group methods. The asymptotic exactness of linear response holds thanks to a cancellation for the scaling limit of the correlations that is reminiscent of bosonization, and which is derived rigorously using emergent chiral Ward identities.



1
Introduction

We consider a system of interacting fermions on a one-dimensional lattice
\Lambda_{L}
of length
L
, with periodic boundary conditions,
\Lambda_{L}=\mathbb{Z}/L\mathbb{Z}
. At equilibrium, the Gibbs state of the system is:

\rho_{\beta,L}=\frac{e^{-\beta(\mathcal{H}-\mu\mathcal{N})}}{\Tr_{\mathcal{F}_{L}}e^{-\beta(\mathcal{H}-\mu\mathcal{N})}}

(1.1)

where
\mathcal{H}
is the many-body Hamiltonian acting on the fermionic Fock space
\mathcal{F}_{L}
(to be specified below), and
\mathcal{N}
is the number operator. The parameters
\beta
and
\mu
are respectively the inverse temperature and the chemical potential; we will consider the case in which
\beta,L
will eventually be taken to infinity, and no uniform spectral gap separates
\mu
from the spectrum of
\mathcal{H}
.

From a physics viewpoint, we are interested in describing the transport properties of
1d
metallic systems. In order to generate a nontrivial charge transport, we shall evolve the equilibrium state of the system with a time-dependent Hamiltonian
\mathcal{H}(\eta t)=\mathcal{H}+e^{\eta t}\mathcal{P}
:

i\partial_{t}\rho(t)=[\mathcal{H}(\eta t),\rho(t)]\;,\qquad t\leq 0

(1.2)

where
\eta>0
is the adiabatic parameter, to be sent to zero after the limit
\beta,L\to\infty
. Given a local observable
\mathcal{O}
, a simple application of Duhamel formula yields the following expression for the variation of the expectation value at first order in the perturbation:

\Tr\mathcal{O}\rho(0)-\Tr\mathcal{O}\rho(-\infty)=-i\int_{-\infty}^{0}dt\,e^{\eta t}\langle[\mathcal{O},\tau_{t}(\mathcal{P})]\rangle_{\beta,L}+\text{higher orders in $\mathcal{P}$}

(1.3)

where
\tau_{t}(\mathcal{P})=e^{i\mathcal{H}t}\mathcal{P}e^{-i\mathcal{H}t}
. The first term in the right-hand side of (
1.3
) is called Kubo formula, and it expresses the linear response of the system. Kubo formula is widely used in condensed matter physics, and it is a nontrivial mathematical challenge to justify its validity: that is, to show that the higher order terms are indeed subleading, uniformly in the system’s size and as
\eta\to 0^{+}
.

For gapped many-body systems, that is in cases in which
\mu
belongs to a spectral gap of
\mathcal{H}
uniformly in the system’s size, Kubo formula has been rigorously justified at zero temperature and in the thermodynamic limit, as a consequence of the many-body adiabatic theorem
[
4
,
37
,
41
]
, see
[
30
]
for a review and further references. The method applies to general,
d
-dimensional lattice models, for a large class of local many-body Hamiltonians with a spectral gap above the ground state. In cases relevant for the quantum Hall effect, one can actually prove that Kubo formula is exact
[
5
,
42
]
: all power law corrections to linear response are zero, which contributes to the explanation of the astonishing precision of the observed quantization of the Hall conductivity. A different approach to the validity of Kubo formula, based on a complex deformation from real to imaginary times, and on cluster expansion methods, has been obtained in
[
29
]
. This work will play an important role in the present paper.

Much less is known about the validity of linear response for gapless many-body systems. There, Kubo formula is still widely used in applications, however with less control on its rigorous validity. In contrast to the gapped case, no adiabatic theorem is available for the dynamics of the ground state of the system. However, one might still have that Kubo formula holds true, in a physically relevant scaling regime. In the case of a finite system coupled to external leads at thermal equilibrium, the related problem of proving convergence to a non-equilibrium steady state (and proving the validity of Kubo formula) has been studied in the literature. Rigorous results have been proved under suitable spectral assumptions, for non-interacting
[
2
,
3
,
38
]
and also interacting
[
21
,
31
,
36
,
18
]
systems.

Here we shall focus on extended, closed systems. In the absence of many-body interactions, the work
[
40
]
proved the validity of Kubo formula for
1d
systems and for the edge modes of
2d
systems, for time-dependent perturbations that are weak and slowly varying in space and in time. The observable
\mathcal{O}
is chosen to be the density or the current density operator, which are related by a lattice continuity equation. Restricting for simplicity to the
1d
case, the time-dependent Hamiltonian is:

\mathcal{H}_{0}(\eta t)=\sum_{x,y\in\Lambda_{L}}\sum_{\rho,\rho^{\prime}\in S_{M}}a^{*}_{x,\rho}H_{\rho\rho^{\prime}}(x,y)a_{y,\rho^{\prime}}+e^{\eta t}\sum_{x\in\Lambda_{L}}\sum_{\rho\in S_{M}}\theta\mu(\theta x)a^{*}_{x,\rho}a_{x,\rho}\;,

(1.4)

where
H(x,y)\equiv H(x-y)
is finite-ranged,
\mu(x)
is a smooth, fast decaying function, and we allow for
M
internal degrees of freedom, whose set of labels is
S_{M}=\{1,\ldots,M\}
. It is assumed that, at the Fermi level, the energy bands of the Hamiltonian
H
cross the Fermi energy transversally, at the Fermi points
k_{F}^{\omega}
,
\omega=1,\ldots,N_{f}
.

The scaling of the perturbation is chosen so that its position space
\ell^{1}
norm is finite. Still, due to the fact that the system is effectively exposed to the perturbation for a time-scale of order
1/\eta
, proving the validity of Kubo formula is a nontrivial task. To achieve this goal, the work
[
40
]
used a mapping of the real-time Duhamel series into an imaginary-time cumulant expansion, proved in
[
29
]
. The advantage of this representation is that the oscillatory behavior of real-time correlations translates into (mild) decay properties in imaginary time, which are easier to exploit to control time integrals and spatial sums. In order to prove the validity and asymptotic exactness of Kubo formula, the work
[
40
]
relied on a cancellation for the density-density cumulants of the scaling limit of the model, which describes massless relativistic fermions in
1+1
dimensions. This cancellation is related to bosonization, and it becomes easier to detect once the time-dependent perturbation theory is formulated in imaginary times. Furthermore, the work
[
40
]
proved convergence of the Duhamel expansion without a smallness assumption on
\mu(\cdot)
: without this improvement, the
m
-th order expansion in the Duhamel series (
1.3
) would be estimated proportionally to
\theta^{m-1}\times\|\hat{\mu}\|^{m-1}_{\ell^{1}}\times(1/\eta)^{2-m}
(with
\hat{\mu}(p)
the Fourier transform of
\mu(x)
), which implies convergence for
\theta/\eta
bounded and for
\|\hat{\mu}\|_{\ell_{1}}
small enough. Instead, the improved analysis of
[
40
]
allows to extract a factor
1/m!
in the estimate of the correlator, and a factor
\eta^{\gamma}
with
\gamma>0
due to the cancellation for the scaling limit. Taking into account these improvements,
[
40
]
allows to prove that the higher order contributions to linear response are summable and vanishingly small as
\eta,\theta\to 0
, with
0<|\theta/\eta|\leq C\left|\log\eta\right|
.

The goal of the present paper is to extend
[
40
]
to the case of weakly interacting fermions. We focus on one-dimensional fermions, but we believe that, with some extra effort, the analysis could be extended to study edge modes of weakly interacting
2d
systems. The time-dependent Hamiltonian is:

\mathcal{H}(\eta t)=\mathcal{H}_{0}(\eta t)+\lambda\sum_{x,y\in\Lambda_{L}}\sum_{\rho,\rho^{\prime}\in S_{M}}a^{*}_{x,\rho}a_{x,\rho}w(x-y)a^{*}_{y,\rho^{\prime}}a_{y,\rho^{\prime}}\;,

(1.5)

where
w(\cdot)
is a finite-ranged two-body potential, and
\mathcal{H}_{0}(\eta t)
is as in (
1.4
). The single-particle Hamiltonian
H
is taken as in
[
40
]
, with the further requirement that, in the
2
-body scattering, the conservation of Fermi momenta only holds with momenta equal in pairs (elastic scattering); this is generically true for spinless fermions in the absence of special symmetries. An example of
H
that fits the setting is the case of the lattice Laplacian in
1d
, for spinless fermions, for chemical potential
\mu
in the spectrum away from band edges.

We consider the adiabatic protocol (
1.2
). The main result of the present paper is the proof of validity of Kubo formula for the expectation of the density and of the current density operator, as
\eta\to 0^{+}
, taken after
\beta,L\to\infty
, as in
[
40
]
. More precisely, we prove that, denoting by
j_{0,x}
the density operator and by
j_{1,x}
the current density operator (see Eqs. (
2.34
), (
2.35
)), for
\theta/\eta
bounded and for
|\lambda|
small enough:

\Tr j_{\nu,x}\rho(0)-\Tr j_{\nu,x}\rho_{\beta,L}=-i\int_{-\infty}^{0}dt\,e^{\eta t}\langle[j_{\nu,x},\tau_{t}(\mathcal{P})]\rangle_{\beta,L}+O(\theta\eta^{\gamma})+O\bigg(\frac{\theta}{\eta^{3}\beta}\bigg)\;,

(1.6)

where
\gamma>0
and where all error terms are bounded uniformly in
L
. Since the first term is of order
\theta
, Eq. (
1.6
) proves the exactness of linear response as
\beta,L\to\infty
followed by
\eta,\theta\to 0
. It is important to emphasize that
|\theta|,\eta,|\lambda|
are chosen small uniformly in
\beta,L
; however, a technical limitation of the proof is that the range of allowed coupling constants
\lambda
shrinks to zero as
|\theta|/\eta\to\infty
.

Furthermore, the linear response can be explicitly evaluated, in terms of the renormalized Fermi velocities of the quasi-particles; see Theorem
2.6
for the explicit expression. For a general number of Fermi points, to the best of our knowledge these expressions are rigorously derived in the present paper for the first time.

The proof follows the strategy of
[
40
]
, however in a much more difficult context, due to the presence of the many-body interaction. The strategy of
[
40
]
was based on an explicit analysis of the Euclidean correlation functions of the system, performed using the quasi-freeness of the state. Here, the correlation functions of the system are not explicit, since the many-body system is interacting. In order to deal with this issue, we use rigorous renormalization group methods, that allow to express the Euclidean correlation functions of the system at equilibrium in terms of a convergent renormalized expansion. These RG methods have been applied to a wide class of statistical mechanics and condensed matter systems in the last three decades, see
[
34
]
for a review. The class of models considered in the present paper belongs to the (multichannel) Luttinger liquid universality class: the Luttinger liquid is a
1+1
dimensional QFT that describes interacting massless chiral fermions, and it has special integrability features
[
33
]
. The rigorous RG analysis of
1d
quantum systems belonging to this universality class has been pioneered in
[
14
]
, for spinless fermions, and in
[
16
]
for spinful fermions. These works relied on the exact solvability of the Luttinger model, which represented a limitation of the approach. Such restriction has been removed in
[
12
,
13
]
, via a different strategy, based on the combination of Ward identities and Schwinger-Dyson equations, that ultimately allows to control the flow of all RG couplings without relying on the exact solution. The outcome is a construction of a nontrivial RG fixed point, characterized by anomalous scaling exponents. In the applications to condensed matter physics, the method has been used to compute response functions for interacting
1d
systems, and to prove the validity of the Haldane relations connecting anomalous exponents and transport coefficients,
[
8
,
9
]
. More recently, these techniques have been used to compute edge transport coefficients for interacting
2d
topological insulators, and to prove the quantization of the edge conductance,
[
1
,
35
]
.

The technical core of the present paper is a renormalization group analysis of the
m
-point density-density and current-density Euclidean correlation functions for the interacting
1d
system at equilibrium. Similar analyses have been performed
e.g.
in
[
7
]
, for the Thirring model, and in
[
23
]
, for the interacting Ising model. With respect to these works, here we obtain an improved estimate for the Euclidean correlations for the special external momenta compatible with our adiabatic protocol (after the complex deformation to imaginary times, the temporal component of all independent incoming momenta of the Euclidean correlations is equal to
\eta
), which ultimately allows to prove the summability of the Duhamel expansion without any smallness requirement on the perturbation
\mu(\cdot)
. Furthermore, we extract the scaling limit contribution from the general
m
-point correlation, and we prove that it is vanishing. This step was done via an explicit computation in
[
40
]
, and it is here derived via the RG analysis of the scaling limit of the model. A similar cancellation has been derived in the past for the Thirring model or the Luttinger model
[
7
,
28
]
. Here we extend the cancellation to the multichannel Luttinger liquid universality class. As a technical note, we only need to establish the cancellation for momentum-space correlations with momenta compatible with the adiabatic protocol, which is easier than working in configuration space as in
[
7
,
28
]
. Finally, the explicit form of the susceptibility and of the conductance is determined using lattice Ward identities, following a strategy similar to
[
35
]
.

As a future perspective, it would be very interesting to connect the results and the methods used in the present paper with the derivation of hydrodynamic models for the non-equilibrium dynamics of integrable systems, a subject on which a lot of progress has been obtained in recent years starting from
[
15
,
39
]
, see
[
19
,
20
]
for reviews.

The paper is structured as follows. In Section
2
we define the model, in Fock space, and we state our main result, Theorem
2.6
. In Section
3
we review the Wick rotation of the Duhamel series, proved in
[
29
]
. In Section
4
we discuss the renormalization group analysis of the model, which takes as starting point the Grassmann integral representation of the theory. Until Section
4.6
, the analysis is similar to previous applications of rigorous RG methods
[
1
,
35
]
. Then, in Section
4.6
we discuss the RG analysis of the multi-point correlation functions, and their improved bounds, which allow to prove summability of the Duhamel expansion. These bounds rely on the Gallavotti-Nicolò tree expansion, reviewed in Section
4.5
. In Section
5
we define the reference model, and we prove the Ward identity for the
m
-point density correlations, which allows to prove the vanishing of all correlations with
m\geq 3
. In Section
6
we prove our main result, by comparing the renormalized expansions of lattice and reference model, extracting the cancellation of the reference model, and estimating the remainder taking into account the improvement of Section
4.6
. Also, we compute the susceptibility and the charge conductance, using the explicit form of the scaling limit, and lattice Ward identities to fix the form of the non-universal part. Finally, in Appendix
A
we collect the details of the proof of the vanishing of the density cumulants of the reference model of order higher than two.

This work has been carried out under the auspices of the GNFM of INdAM.

2
Lattice fermions

2.1
Hamiltonian and Gibbs state

Let
L\in 2\mathbb{N}+1
. We consider fermions on a one-dimensional lattice

\Gamma_{L}=\left[-\left\lfloor\frac{L}{2}\right\rfloor,\left\lfloor\frac{L}{2}\right\rfloor\right]\cap\mathbb{Z}

endowed with periodic boundary conditions. We shall allow for internal degrees of freedom,
e.g.
the spin, and we shall denote by
S_{M}=\{1,\ldots,M\}
the set of their labels. We shall use the notation
\Lambda_{L}=\Gamma_{L}\times S_{M}
, which we can view as a decorated
1d
lattice. Given
x\in\Gamma_{L}
and
\rho\in S_{M}
, we shall denote by
{\bf x}=(x,\rho)
the corresponding point in
\Lambda_{L}
. Further, we will make use of the following distance on
\Gamma_{L}
:

|x-y|_{L}=\min_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}|x-y+nL|\;.

(2.1)

Let
H
be a single-particle Hamiltonian on
\Lambda_{L}
. That is,
H
is a self-adjoint matrix, with entries given by
H({\bf x};{\bf y})\equiv H_{\rho\rho^{\prime}}(x;y)
. We shall suppose that the Hamiltonian is finite-ranged, and we shall assume that the Hamiltonian
H
is the periodization of an Hamiltonian
H^{\infty}
defined over
\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}\times S_{M})
:

H_{\rho\rho^{\prime}}(x;y)=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}H^{\infty}_{\rho\rho^{\prime}}(x+nL;y)\qquad\text{for $x,y\in\Gamma_{L}$.}

(2.2)

Furthermore, we will assume
H^{\infty}
, and hence
H
, to be translation-invariant:

H_{\rho\rho^{\prime}}^{\infty}(x;y)=H_{\rho\rho^{\prime}}^{\infty}(x+z;y+z)\qquad\text{for all $z\in\mathbb{Z}$}.

(2.3)

A natural example is the lattice Laplacian:

(H^{\infty}\psi)(x)=t(\psi_{x-1}+\psi_{x+1}-2\psi_{x})\;,

(2.4)

with
t\in\mathbb{R}
being the hopping parameter.

Translation-invariance allows to conveniently express the Hamiltonian
H
in momentum space. To this end, let us introduce the set of quasi-momenta compatible with the periodic boundary conditions:

B_{L}=\Big\{k=\frac{2\pi}{L}n\,\Big|\,0\leq n\leq L-1\Big\}\;.

(2.5)

The set
B_{L}
is the (discretized) Brillouin zone. Given a function
f
on
\Gamma_{L}
, we define its Bloch transform as:

\hat{f}(k)=\sum_{x\in\Gamma_{L}}e^{-ikx}f(x)\qquad\text{for all $k\in B_{L}$.}

(2.6)

This relation can be inverted, as:

f(x)=\frac{1}{L}\sum_{k\in B_{L}}e^{ikx}\hat{f}(k)\qquad\text{for all $x\in\Gamma_{L}$.}

(2.7)

As a function on
\frac{2\pi}{L}\mathbb{Z}
, the function
\hat{f}(k)
is periodic with period
2\pi
. Thus, it is natural to introduce the following norm for quasi-momenta:

|k|_{\mathbb{T}}=\min_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}|k+2\pi n|\qquad\text{for all $k\in B_{L}$.}

(2.8)

Next, we define the Bloch Hamiltonian
\hat{H}(k)
as the
M\times M
Hermitian matrix with entries:

\hat{H}_{\rho\rho^{\prime}}(k)=\sum_{x\in\Gamma_{L}}e^{-ikx}H_{\rho\rho^{\prime}}(x;0)\;.

(2.9)

Observe that, because of equation (
2.3
),
\hat{H}(k)
is the restriction to
B_{L}
of
\hat{H}^{\infty}(k)
, defined on the circle
\mathbb{T}
of length
2\pi
.

We shall make the following assumptions on the spectrum of the Bloch Hamiltonian and on the chemical potential
\mu\in\mathbb{R}
.

Assumption 2.1

(Low energy spectrum.)
.

There exists
\Delta>0
such that the following points are true.

(i)

There exist
N_{f}\in\mathbb{N}
disjoint sets
I_{\omega}\subset\mathbb{T}
, labelled by
\omega=1,\ldots,N_{f}
, and strictly monotone smooth functions
e_{\omega}:I_{\omega}\to\mathbb{R}
such that:

\sigma(H^{\infty})\cap(\mu-\Delta,\mu+\Delta)=\bigcup_{\omega=1}^{N_{f}}\mathrm{Ran}(e_{\omega})\;.

(2.10)

The functions
e_{\omega}
are the eigenvalue branches of the Bloch Hamiltonian
\hat{H}^{\infty}
, crossing the Fermi level
\mu
.

(ii)

We introduce the
\omega
-Fermi point
k_{F}^{\omega}\in I_{\omega}
and the
\omega
-Fermi velocity
v_{\omega}
as the real numbers satisfying:

e_{\omega}(k_{F}^{\omega})=\mu\;,\qquad v_{\omega}=\partial_{k}e_{\omega}(k_{F}^{\omega})\;.

(2.11)

Notice that
v_{\omega}\neq 0
, by the strict monotonicity of
e_{\omega}
. The set of Fermi points
\{k_{F}^{\omega}\}_{\omega=1,\ldots,N_{f}}
will be referred to as the (discrete)
Fermi surface
of the system.

(iii)

For any
\omega=1,\dots,N_{f}
, and
k\in I_{\omega}
,
e_{\omega}(k)
is a non-degenerate eigenvalue of
\hat{H}^{\infty}(k)
. In particular, the Fermi points
k_{F}^{\omega}
,
\omega=1,\ldots,N_{f}
are all different.

(iv)

For any quadruple
(\omega_{1},\omega_{2},\omega_{3},\omega_{4})
, we have that

k^{\omega_{1}}_{F}-k^{\omega_{2}}_{F}=k^{\omega_{3}}_{F}-k^{\omega_{4}}_{F}\qquad\mod 2\pi

(2.12)

only if
\omega_{1}=\omega_{2}
and
\omega_{3}=\omega_{4}
, or if
\omega_{1}=\omega_{3}
and
\omega_{2}=\omega_{4}
.

Remark 2.2
.

(i)

A paradigmatic example satisfying the assumptions is (minus) the Laplacian on
\mathbb{Z}
, for spinless fermions. The spectrum is described by:

\varepsilon(k)=2t(1-\cos(k))\;,

(2.13)

with
t>0
the hopping parameter, so that
N_{f}=2
for any
\mu\in(0,4t)
.

(ii)

In general, it is a well-known fact that short-ranged one-dimensional lattice models present necessarily an even number
N_{f}
of Fermi points, with net chirality equal to zero:
\sum_{\omega=1}^{N_{f}}v_{\omega}/|v_{\omega}|=0
.

(iii)

Eq. (
2.12
) can be viewed as an elastic scattering assumption for the Fermi momenta of the system. It is generically true for spinless fermions, in the absence of special symmetry properties of the spectrum.

Next, we will introduce the class of many-body interacting systems we will consider in this paper, in the grand-canonical setting. To this end, it is convenient to lift the many particle systems to the fermionic Fock space
\mathcal{F}
,

\mathcal{F}=\mathbb{C}\oplus\bigoplus_{n\geq 1}\ell^{2}(\Lambda_{L})^{\wedge n}\;,

(2.14)

where
\wedge
is the antisymmetric tensor product. We shall use the notation
{\bf x}=(x,\rho)
for points in
\Lambda_{L}
, with
x\in\Gamma_{L}
and
\rho\in S_{M}
, and we shall denote by
a^{*}_{{\bf x}},a_{{\bf x}}
the usual fermionic creation and annihilation operators, satisfying the canonical anticommutation relations:

\{a_{{\bf x}},a^{*}_{{\bf y}}\}=\delta_{{\bf x},{\bf y}}\;,\qquad\{a_{{\bf x}},a_{{\bf y}}\}=\{a^{*}_{{\bf x}},a^{*}_{{\bf y}}\}=0\;.

(2.15)

Later, it will also be convenient to switch to Fourier space. For any
k\in B_{L}
, we define the Bloch transform of the fermionic operators as:

\hat{a}_{(k,\rho)}=\sum_{x\in\Gamma_{L}}a_{(x,\rho)}e^{-ikx}\;,\qquad\hat{a}^{*}_{(k,\rho)}=\sum_{x\in\Gamma_{L}}a^{*}_{(x,\rho)}e^{ikx}\;.

(2.16)

Equations (
2.16
) can be inverted as, for
(x,\rho)\in\Lambda_{L}
:

a_{(x,\rho)}=\frac{1}{L}\sum_{k\in B_{L}}e^{ikx}\hat{a}_{(k,\rho)}\;,\qquad a^{*}_{(x,\rho)}=\frac{1}{L}\sum_{k\in B_{L}}e^{-ikx}\hat{a}^{*}_{(k,\rho)}\;.

(2.17)

In terms of the creation and annihilation operators, we define the many-body Hamiltonian
\mathcal{H}
acting on
\mathcal{F}
as:

\mathcal{H}=\sum_{{\bf x},{\bf y}\in\Lambda_{L}}a^{*}_{{\bf x}}H({\bf x};{\bf y})a_{{\bf y}}+\lambda\sum_{{\bf x},{\bf y}\in\Lambda_{L}}\Big(n_{{\bf x}}-\frac{1}{2}\Big)w({\bf x};{\bf y})\Big(n_{{\bf y}}-\frac{1}{2}\Big)\;,

(2.18)

where:
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
is the coupling constant,
n_{{\bf x}}=a^{*}_{{\bf x}}a_{{\bf x}}
is the density operator; and
w({\bf x};{\bf y})
is the two-body potential. As for the single-particle Hamiltonian, we will suppose that
w
is translation-invariant, compatible with the periodic boundary conditions, and finite-ranged. Futhermore, for simplicity we will assume that
w({\bf x};{\bf y})\equiv w(x;y)
. That is:

w({\bf x};{\bf y})=\frac{1}{L}\sum_{k\in B_{L}}e^{ik(x-y)}\hat{w}(k)\;,

(2.19)

where
\hat{w}(k)
is the restriction to
B_{L}
of a real-analytic function on
\mathbb{T}
.

Remark 2.3
.

The factors
-1/2
in (
2.18
) amount to a shift of the many-body Hamiltonian by a constant term and by a term proportional to the number operator, which can be reabsorbed in the definition of the chemical potential of the interacting Gibbs state. These factors are introduced to simplify the Grassmann representation of the Gibbs state, discussed later.

Finally, the Gibbs state of the system at inverse temperature
\beta>0
is:

\langle\mathcal{O}\rangle_{\beta,L}=\Tr\mathcal{O}\rho_{\beta,\mu,L}\;,\qquad\rho_{\beta,L}=\frac{e^{-\beta(\mathcal{H}-\mu\mathcal{N})}}{\Tr e^{-\beta(\mathcal{H}-\mu\mathcal{N})}}\;,

(2.20)

with
\mu\in\mathbb{R}
the chemical potential and
\mathcal{N}
the number operator. We shall assume that Assumption
2.1
holds, for our choice of the chemical potential
\mu
. Notice that the Gibbs state depends also on the parameters
\lambda,\mu
, but in order to simplify the notation we have chosen to write explicitly only the dependence on the variables
\beta,L
.

2.2
Dynamics and linear response

Perturbing the system.

We will be interested in the response properties of the system, after exposing it to a time-dependent and slowly varying perturbation. We shall consider time-dependent Hamiltonians of the form:

\mathcal{H}(\eta t)=\mathcal{H}+e^{\eta t}\mathcal{P}\;,

(2.21)

for
\eta>0
and
t\leq 0
. We will consider external perturbations that couple to the density of the system:

\mathcal{P}=\theta\sum_{{\bf x}\in\Lambda_{L}}\mu(\theta x)a^{*}_{{\bf x}}a_{{\bf x}}\;,

(2.22)

where:
\mu(\cdot)
(see Figure
1
) is the restriction to
\Gamma_{L}
of the periodization of a function
\mu_{\infty}(x)
in
\mathbb{R}
such that
\hat{\mu}_{\infty}(p)
has compact support:

\mu(x)=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\mu_{\infty}(x+nL)\;,\qquad\text{for $x\in\Gamma_{L}$.}

(2.23)

Thus,
\hat{\mu}(p)
is
\hat{\mu}_{\infty}(p)
restricted to
p\in(2\pi/L)\mathbb{Z}
. The compact support restriction could be replaced by fast decay in momentum space, but we prefer to work in a slightly more restrictive setting to avoid extra technicalities. Observe that the parameter
\theta
in (
2.22
) defines simultaneously the strength and the space-variation of the perturbation, and will be suitably chosen later on.

Figure 1:
Qualitative representation of the spatial lattice
\Gamma_{L}
and typical form of the rescaled local potential entering the definition of
\mathcal{P}
, equation (
2.22
).

Let
t,s\leq 0
, and let
\mathcal{U}(t;s)
be the unitary group generated by
\mathcal{H}(\eta t)
:

i\partial_{t}\mathcal{U}(t;s)=\mathcal{H}(\eta t)\mathcal{U}(t;s),\qquad\mathcal{U}(s;s)=\mathbbm{1}\;.

(2.24)

Let us consider the evolution of the Gibbs state
\rho_{\beta,L}
,

\rho(t)=\lim_{T\to+\infty}\mathcal{U}(t;-T)\rho_{\beta,L}\mathcal{U}(t;-T)^{*}.

(2.25)

We will be interested in the variation of physical observables under this perturbation protocol,

\Tr\mathcal{O}\rho(t)-\Tr\mathcal{O}\rho_{\beta,L}\;.

(2.26)

We will be interested in the following order of limits: first
L\to\infty
, then
\beta\to\infty
and then
\eta,\theta\to 0^{+}
.
Time-dependent perturbation theory is generated by the Duhamel series, that describes the expansion around the unperturbed evolution in the interaction picture. Let
\tau_{t}(\mathcal{A})
be the Heisenberg evolution of the observable
\mathcal{A}
:

\tau_{t}(\mathcal{A})=e^{i\mathcal{H}t}\mathcal{A}e^{-i\mathcal{H}t}\;.

(2.27)

We have:

\begin{split}\Tr\mathcal{O}\rho(t)-\Tr\mathcal{O}\rho_{\beta,L}&=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}(-i)^{n}\int_{-\infty\leq s_{n}\leq\ldots\leq s_{1}\leq t}d\underline{s}\,e^{\eta(s_{1}+\ldots s_{n})}\\
&\quad\cdot\langle[\cdots[[\tau_{t}(\mathcal{O}),\tau_{s_{1}}(\mathcal{P})],\tau_{s_{2}}(\mathcal{P})]\cdots\tau_{s_{n}}(\mathcal{P})]\rangle_{\beta,L}\;.\end{split}

(2.28)

It is known that, for
\eta>0
and for finite
L
, the expansion in the right-hand side is absolutely convergent. The linear response approximation amounts to the truncation of the above expansion to first order in
\mathcal{P}
:

\Tr\mathcal{O}\rho(t)-\Tr\mathcal{O}\rho_{\beta,L}=-i\int_{-\infty}^{t}ds\,e^{\eta s}\Tr\big[\tau_{t}(\mathcal{O}),\tau_{s}(\mathcal{P})\big]\rho_{\beta,\mu,L}+\text{h.o.t.}\;.

(2.29)

Proving that the error term is small uniformly in
\beta
and
L
is in general a very difficult problem, in particular for gapless interacting models, as in the present work.

Density and current operators.

We will focus on the dynamics of the expectation values of density and current operators, defined as follows. Let:

n_{x}=\sum_{\rho\in S_{M}}n_{(x,\rho)}\equiv\sum_{\rho\in S_{M}}a^{*}_{(x,\rho)}a_{(x,\rho)}\;.

(2.30)

be the (total) density at
x\in\Gamma_{L}
. In order to introduce the current operator, let us consider the time-variation of the density operator:

\begin{split}\partial_{t}\tau_{t}(n_{{\bf x}})&=i\tau_{t}\big([\mathcal{H},n_{{\bf x}}]\big)\\
&=\sum_{{\bf y}\in\Lambda_{L}}\tau_{t}(ia^{*}_{{\bf y}}H({\bf y};{\bf x})a_{{\bf x}}-ia^{*}_{{\bf x}}H({\bf x};{\bf y})a_{{\bf y}})\\
&\equiv\sum_{{\bf y}\in\Lambda_{L}}\tau_{t}(j_{{\bf y},{\bf x}})\;,\end{split}

(2.31)

where
j_{{\bf y},{\bf x}}=ia^{*}_{{\bf y}}H({\bf y};{\bf x})a_{{\bf x}}-ia^{*}_{{\bf x}}H({\bf x};{\bf y})a_{{\bf y}}
is the bond current between
{\bf x}
and
{\bf y}
. Let us derive the conservation law for
n_{x}
. To this end, in (
2.31
) we sum over the internal label
\rho
in
\mathbf{x}=(x,\rho)
, and we introduce the discrete derivative
\text{d}_{x}
as
(\text{d}_{x}f)(x)=f(x)-f(x-1)
to obtain the continuity equation

\partial_{t}\tau_{t}(n_{x})=-\text{d}_{x}\tau_{t}(j_{x})\;,

(2.32)

where we introduced the lattice current
j_{x}
:

\mathrm{d}_{x}j_{x}:=\sum_{y\in\Gamma_{L}}\sum_{\rho,\rho^{\prime}\in S_{M}}j_{(x,\rho),(y,\rho^{\prime})}\;.

(2.33)

Eq. (
2.32
) will have far reaching consequences in our analysis.

It is convenient to rewrite density and current operators in Fourier space. Let
j_{\nu,x}
,
\nu=0,1
, be defined as
j_{0,x}=n_{x}
,
j_{1,x}=j_{x}
. In Fourier space:

j_{\nu,x}=\frac{1}{L}\sum_{p\in B_{L}}e^{ipx}\hat{\jmath}_{\nu,p}\;,\qquad\hat{\jmath}_{\nu,p}=\frac{1}{L}\sum_{k\in B_{L}}(a^{*}_{k-p},\hat{J}_{\nu}(k,p)a_{k})\;,

(2.34)

where we used the shorthand notation
(a^{*},Ja)=\sum_{\rho,\rho^{\prime}}a^{*}_{\rho}J_{\rho\rho^{\prime}}a_{\rho^{\prime}}
. An explicit computation gives:

\hat{J}_{0}(k,p)=1\;,\qquad\hat{J}_{1}(k,p)=i\frac{\hat{H}(k)-\hat{H}(k-p)}{1-e^{-ip}}\;.

(2.35)

Remark 2.4
.

If
H
has range 1, then
j_{1,x}
takes a particularly simple form:

j_{1,x}\equiv j_{x}=\sum_{\rho,\rho^{\prime}\in S_{M}}j_{(x,\rho),(x+1,\rho^{\prime})}\;.

(2.36)

Finally, we define a smeared version of the density and of the current operators as:

j_{\nu}(\mu_{\theta})=\sum_{x\in\Gamma_{L}}j_{\nu,x}\theta\mu(\theta x)\;.

(2.37)

Imaginary time evolution and Euclidean correlations.

For
t\in[0,\beta)
, we define the imaginary time evolution of an observable
\mathcal{O}
as:

\gamma_{t}(\mathcal{O}):=e^{t(\mathcal{H}-\mu\mathcal{N})}\mathcal{O}e^{-t(\mathcal{H}-\mu\mathcal{N})}\;.

(2.38)

Observe that if
[\mathcal{O},\mathcal{N}]=0
, the following identity holds:

\gamma_{t}(\mathcal{O})=\tau_{-it}(\mathcal{O})\;.

(2.39)

The Gibbs state and the imaginary time evolution satisfy the following important identity, which in our finite-dimensional setting is a simple consequence of the cyclicity of the trace:

\langle\gamma_{t}(\mathcal{A})\gamma_{s}(\mathcal{B})\rangle_{\beta,L}=\langle\gamma_{t+\beta}(\mathcal{B})\gamma_{s}(\mathcal{A})\rangle_{\beta,L}\;.

(2.40)

Eq. (
2.40
) is called the Kubo-Martin-Schwinger (KMS) identity, and will play an important role in what follows.

Let
0\leq t_{i}<\beta
, for
i=1,2,\ldots,n
, such that
t_{i}\neq t_{j}
for
i\neq j
. We define the time-ordering of
\gamma_{t_{1}}(a^{\sharp_{1}}_{{\bf x}_{1}}),\ldots,\gamma_{t_{n}}(a^{\sharp_{n}}_{{\bf x}_{n}})
as:

{\bf T}\gamma_{t_{1}}(a^{\sharp_{1}}_{{\bf x}_{1}})\cdots\gamma_{t_{n}}(a^{\sharp_{n}}_{{\bf x}_{n}})=(-1)^{\pi}\gamma_{t_{\pi(1)}}(a^{\sharp_{\pi(1)}}_{{\bf x}_{\pi(1)}})\cdots\gamma_{t_{\pi(n)}}(a^{\sharp_{\pi(n)}}_{{\bf x}_{\pi(n)}})\;,

(2.41)

where
\pi
is the permutation needed in order to bring the times in a decreasing order, from the left, with sign
(-1)^{\pi}
. In case two or more times are equal, the definition (
2.41
) is extended via normal ordering. The above definition extends to all operators on the fermionic Fock space by linearity. In particular, for
\mathcal{O}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{O}_{n}
normal ordered and even in the number of creation and annihilation operators, we have:

{\bf T}\gamma_{t_{1}}(\mathcal{O}_{1})\cdots\gamma_{t_{n}}(\mathcal{O}_{n})=\gamma_{t_{\pi(1)}}(\mathcal{O}_{\pi(1)})\cdots\gamma_{t_{\pi(n)}}(\mathcal{O}_{\pi(n)})\;.

(2.42)

We define the time-ordered Euclidean correlation function as:

\langle{\bf T}\gamma_{t_{1}}(\mathcal{O}_{1})\cdots\gamma_{t_{n}}(\mathcal{O}_{n})\rangle_{\beta,L}\;.

(2.43)

From the definition of fermionic time-ordering, and from the KMS identity, it is not difficult to check that:

\langle{\bf T}\gamma_{t_{1}}(\mathcal{O}_{1})\cdots\gamma_{\beta}(\mathcal{O}_{k})\cdots\gamma_{t_{n}}(\mathcal{O}_{n})\rangle_{\beta,L}=(\pm 1)\langle{\bf T}\gamma_{t_{1}}(\mathcal{O}_{1})\cdots\gamma_{0}(\mathcal{O}_{k})\cdots\gamma_{t_{n}}(\mathcal{O}_{n})\rangle_{\beta,L}\;;

(2.44)

in the special case in which the operators involve an even number of creation and annihilation operators, which is the relevant one for our analysis, the overall sign is
+1
. The property (
2.44
) is used to extend in a periodic (sign
+1
) or antiperiodic (sign
-1
) way the correlation functions to all times
t_{i}\in\mathbb{R}
. From now on, we shall always assume that this extension has been taken.

Next, we define the connected time-ordered Euclidean correlation functions, or time-ordered Euclidean cumulants, as:

\langle{\bf T}\gamma_{t_{1}}(\mathcal{O}_{1});\cdots;\gamma_{t_{n}}(\mathcal{O}_{n})\rangle_{\beta,L}=\frac{\partial^{n}}{\partial\zeta_{1}\cdots\partial\zeta_{n}}\log\Big\{1+\sum_{I\subseteq\{1,2,\ldots,n\}}\zeta(I)\langle{\bf T}\mathcal{O}(I)\rangle_{\beta,L}\Big\}\Big|_{\zeta_{i}=0}

(2.45)

where
I
is a non-empty ordered subset of
\{1,2,\ldots,n\}
,
\zeta(I)=\prod_{i\in I}\zeta_{i}
and
\mathcal{O}(I)=\prod_{i\in I}\gamma_{t_{i}}(\mathcal{O}_{i})
. For
n=1
, this definition reduces to
\langle{\bf T}\gamma_{t_{1}}(\mathcal{O}_{1})\rangle\equiv\langle\gamma_{t_{1}}(\mathcal{O}_{1})\rangle=\langle\mathcal{O}_{1}\rangle
, while for
n=2
one gets
\langle{\bf T}\gamma_{t_{1}}(\mathcal{O}_{1});\gamma_{t_{2}}(\mathcal{O}_{2})\rangle=\langle{\bf T}\gamma_{t_{1}}(\mathcal{O}_{1})\gamma_{t_{2}}(\mathcal{O}_{2})\rangle-\langle{\bf T}\gamma_{t_{1}}(\mathcal{O}_{1})\rangle\langle{\bf T}\gamma_{t_{2}}(\mathcal{O}_{2})\rangle
. Recall the following relation between correlation functions and connected correlation functions:

\langle{\bf T}\gamma_{t_{1}}(\mathcal{O}_{1})\cdots\gamma_{t_{n}}(\mathcal{O}_{n})\rangle_{\beta,L}=\sum_{P}\prod_{J\in P}\langle{\bf T}\gamma_{t_{j_{1}}}(\mathcal{O}_{j_{1}});\cdots;\gamma_{t_{j_{|J|}}}(\mathcal{O}_{j_{|J|}})\rangle_{\beta,L}\;,

where
P
is the set of all partitions of
\{1,2,\ldots,n\}
into ordered subsets, and
J
is an element of the partition
P
,
J=\{j_{1},\ldots,j_{|J|}\}
.

The next result establishes the connection between the real time perturbation theory generated by the Duhamel series and the Euclidean correlation functions, for special choices of the adiabatic parameter
\eta
.

Proposition 2.5

(Wick rotation)
.

Let
\eta_{\beta}\in\frac{2\pi}{\beta}\mathbb{N}_{+}
. Let
\mathcal{O}
and
\mathcal{P}
be such that
[\mathcal{O},\mathcal{N}]=[\mathcal{P},\mathcal{N}]=0
. Then, the following identity holds true:

\begin{split}&\int_{-\infty\leq s_{n}\leq\ldots\leq s_{1}\leq t}d\underline{s}\,e^{\eta_{\beta}(s_{1}+\ldots s_{n})}\langle[\cdots[[\tau_{t}(\mathcal{O}),\tau_{s_{1}}(\mathcal{P})],\tau_{s_{2}}(\mathcal{P})]\cdots\tau_{s_{n}}(\mathcal{P})]\rangle_{\beta,L}\\
&=\frac{(-i)^{n}e^{n\eta_{\beta}t}}{n!}\int_{[0,\beta)^{n}}d\underline{s}\,e^{-i\eta_{\beta}(s_{1}+\ldots s_{n})}\langle{\bf T}\gamma_{s_{1}}(\mathcal{P});\gamma_{s_{2}}(\mathcal{P});\cdots;\gamma_{s_{n}}(\mathcal{P});\mathcal{O}\rangle_{\beta,L}\;.\end{split}

(2.46)

The identity (
2.46
) has been proved in
[
29
]
. It is the starting point of the analysis of the present paper: the right-hand side of the identity can be studied with field-theoretic methods. In particular, for gapless cases, like the ones considered in the present work, the right-hand side of (
2.46
) can be analyzed via rigorous renormalization group methods. Observe that equation (
2.46
) only holds for special values of the adiabatic parameters, corresponding to bosonic Matsubara frequencies. For
\beta
large enough, an approximation argument based on Lieb-Robinson bounds allows to study the dynamics associated with a general
\eta>0
with these special adiabatic parameters, up to subleading error terms (see below).

2.3
Main result

For
x\in\Gamma_{L}
, let us define the full response of the density and of the current operators as:

\chi^{\beta,L}_{\nu}(x;\eta,\theta):=\frac{1}{\theta}\Big(\Tr j_{\nu,x}\rho(0)-\Tr j_{\nu,x}\rho_{\beta,L}\Big)\qquad\nu=0,1.

(2.47)

The quantity
\chi^{\beta,L}_{0}
is the susceptibility, while the quantity
\chi^{\beta,L}_{1}
is the conductance. The next theorem determines the structure of these response functions, as
\beta,L\to\infty
, and for
\theta,\eta,\lambda
small enough. In the following, we shall denote by
\eta_{\beta}
the best approximation of
\eta
in
\frac{2\pi}{\beta}\mathbb{N}
, such that
\eta_{\beta}\geq\eta
.

Theorem 2.6

(Main result)
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be of the form (
2.18
), and assume that
H
satisfies Assumption
2.1
. Let
\mathcal{H}(\eta t)
be as in (
2.21
)-(
2.23
). Let
a=|\theta|/\eta
.

Validity of linear response.

There exist constants
\gamma,\eta_{0},\bar{\lambda}>0
such that, for all
0\leq\eta<\eta_{0}
and for all
|\lambda|<\bar{\lambda}
, linear response is valid for
\beta,L
large enough,
i.e.

\chi^{\beta,L}_{\nu}(x;\eta,\theta)=\chi^{\beta,L,\mathrm{lin}}_{\nu}(x;\eta,\theta)+O(\eta^{\gamma})+O\bigg(\frac{1}{\eta^{3}\beta}\bigg)\;,

with
\chi^{\beta,L,\mathrm{lin}}_{\nu}
is the first order term in the Duhamel expansion (
2.28
), after replacing
\eta
with
\eta_{\beta}
.

Structure of linear response.
The linear response can be written as follows:

\begin{split}\chi^{\beta,L,\mathrm{lin}}_{\nu}(x;\eta,\theta)&=\chi^{\mathrm{lin}}_{\nu}(x;\eta,\theta)+O(\beta^{-1})+O(L^{-1})\\
\chi^{\mathrm{lin}}_{\nu}(x;\theta,\eta)&=-\sum_{\omega,\omega^{\prime}=1}^{N_{f}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\hat{\mu}_{\infty}(q)\,e^{i\theta qx}\,\mathcal{K}^{\nu}_{\omega\omega^{\prime}}(q;\lambda)\frac{dq}{2\pi}+O(\eta^{\tilde{\gamma}})\;,\end{split}

(2.48)

for some
\tilde{\gamma}>0
. The matrix-valued response function
\mathcal{K}^{\nu}
is given as follows. There exist an analytic function
v:(-\bar{\lambda},\bar{\lambda})\to\mathrm{GL}(N_{f})
, called the matrix of renormalized Fermi velocities, such that

v_{\omega\omega^{\prime}}(\lambda)=\delta_{\omega\omega^{\prime}}v_{\omega}(\lambda)\;,\qquad\qquad v_{\omega}(\lambda)=v_{\omega}+O(\lambda)\;,

and an analytic function
\Lambda:(-\bar{\lambda},\bar{\lambda})\to\mathrm{GL}(N_{f})
with

\Lambda^{T}(\lambda)=\Lambda(\lambda)\;,\qquad\qquad\Lambda_{\omega\omega^{\prime}}(\lambda)=\begin{cases}0&\omega=\omega^{\prime}\\
\lambda+O(\lambda^{2})&\omega\neq\omega^{\prime}\;,\end{cases}

such that the matrix
\mathcal{K}^{\nu}
is given by

\mathcal{K}^{\nu}(q;\lambda)=\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}(q;\lambda)\frac{v^{\nu}(\lambda)}{2\pi|v(\lambda)|}\frac{v(\lambda)q}{-i/a+v(\lambda)q}\mathfrak{K}^{\nu}(\lambda)\;,

with
v^{\nu}:=\delta_{\nu,0}\mathbbm{1}+\delta_{\nu,1}v
, and
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}
and
\mathfrak{K}^{\nu}
defined as

\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}(q)=\left(1-\frac{1}{4\pi|v|}\Lambda\right)\left(1+\frac{1}{4\pi|v|}\frac{i/a+vq}{-i/a+vq}\Lambda\right)^{-1}\;,

\begin{split}\quad\quad\mathfrak{K}^{0}\equiv\mathbbm{1}_{N_{f}}\;,\qquad\qquad\mathfrak{K}^{1}:=\mathrm{sgn\,}v\,\frac{1+(4\pi|v|)^{-1}\Lambda}{1-(4\pi|v|)^{-1}\Lambda}\,\mathrm{sgn\,}v\;.\end{split}

Remark 2.7
.

(i)

The above theorem justifies the validity of linear response for interacting one-dimensional systems in the absence of a spectral gap. At zero temperature and as
\eta\to 0^{+}
the only contribution surviving to the response of the system is the one associated to Kubo formula, which can be explicitly evaluated.

(ii)

A technical limitation of the proof is that the range of allowed
\lambda
is not uniform in
a=|\theta|/\eta
. In particular, our current estimates imply that
\bar{\lambda}
shrinks to zero as
a\to\infty
.

(iii)

Suppose that
a\to 0
. Then, the above result shows that the response of the system at zero temperature is trivial as
\eta\to 0^{+}
, since:

\chi^{\mathrm{lin}}_{\nu}(x,\eta,\theta)=O(a)\;.

(2.49)

(iii)

Considering non-interacting systems,
i.e.
for
\lambda=0
, we notice that both
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}
and
\mathfrak{K}^{\nu}
become the identity matrix, and the Fermi velocities reduce to the free ones, so that

\mathcal{K}^{\nu}(q;0)=\frac{v^{\nu}(0)}{2\pi|v(0)|}\frac{v(0)q}{-i/a+v(0)q}\;,

which is in agreement with the result obtained in
[
40
]
. Thus, the many-body interaction modifies the response in two concurrent ways: by dressing the velocities of the infrared modes labelled by the Fermi points, and by mixing the contribution of each mode via
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}
and
\mathfrak{K}^{\nu}
.

(iv)

For non-interacting systems, a similar result has been obtained in
[
40
]
. The analysis of
[
40
]
was based on a careful analysis of the real-time Duhamel expansion after Wick rotation. Here, such direct analysis is not possible, due to the fact that the theory is interacting. We will instead resort to rigorous renormalization group methods, that will allow to prove essentially optimal estimates on the imaginary-time correlators.

(v)

At the price of a straightforward approximation argument, carried out in the non-interacting setting in
[
40
]
, the result could be extended to the case in which
\hat{\mu}_{\infty}(p)
decays fast enough, rather that assuming that
\hat{\mu}_{\infty}(p)
is compactly supported in
p
. We prefer to avoid this extra technical step, which we believe would not add anything substantial to the main result of this work.

Remark 2.8

(The case of two chiralities)
.

In the case of two opposite Fermi points with counterpropagating velocities the expression in (
2.48
) drastically simplifies. This case is relevant for instance when
H
is the nearest-neighbour lattice Laplacian. We will show how the general expression (
2.48
) reproduces known rigorous results, see
e.g.

[
12
,
9
]
. For simplicity, we shall evaluate it for
a=1
, that is
\theta=\eta
; the expression for
a\neq 1
will be simply given by substituting
q\to aq
in the response for
a=1
.

The dispersion relation is
\varepsilon(k)=2(1-\cos k)
, and for any choice of
\mu\in(0,4)
we have
N_{f}=2
distinct free Fermi points
k_{F}^{+}=-k_{F}^{-}
with opposite free Fermi velocities
v_{\pm}=\pm v_{0}
, with
v_{0}=2\sin k_{F}^{+}>0
. Since spatial
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-parity is a symmetry of the model, in this case the renormalized parameters needed are just two, namely
v_{\star}(\lambda)=v_{0}+O(\lambda)
,
\lambda_{\star}(\lambda)=\lambda+O(\lambda^{2})
, so that the matrices
v
and
\Lambda
introduced in Theorem
2.6
become

v=v_{\star}\sigma_{3}\qquad\qquad\Lambda=\lambda_{\star}\sigma_{1}

with
\sigma_{1}
,
\sigma_{3}
the first and the third Pauli matrices.
For simplicity, let us set

b:=\frac{\lambda_{\star}}{4\pi v_{\star}}\quad\qquad\mathfrak{b}_{\pm}(q):=\frac{i\pm v_{\star}q}{-i\pm v_{\star}q}\quad\qquad B(q):=b\begin{pmatrix}0&\mathfrak{b}_{+}(q)\\
\mathfrak{b}_{-}(q)&0\end{pmatrix}\;.

Then, the response matrices are given by

\mathcal{K}^{0}(q)=\frac{1}{2\pi v_{\star}}(1-b\sigma_{1})\frac{1}{1+B(q)}\frac{v_{\star}q\sigma_{3}}{-i+v_{\star}q\sigma_{3}},\qquad\mathcal{K}^{1}(q)=v_{\star}\mathcal{K}^{0}(q)\frac{1+b\sigma_{1}}{1-b\sigma_{1}}\sigma_{3}\;.

(2.50)

Let us compute
\mathcal{K}^{0}
first. By using the properties of
\sigma_{3}
, and employing the fact that
\det[1+B(q)]=1-b^{2}\mathfrak{b}_{+}(q)\mathfrak{b}_{-}(q)\equiv 1-b^{2}
, we have

\frac{1}{1+B(q)}=\frac{1-B(q)}{1-b^{2}}\qquad\qquad\frac{v_{\star}q\sigma_{3}}{-i+v_{\star}q\sigma_{3}}=\frac{v_{\star}q}{1+v^{2}_{\star}q^{2}}(i\sigma_{3}+v_{\star}q)\;,

so that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{1+B(q)}\frac{v_{\star}q\sigma_{3}}{-i+v_{\star}q\sigma_{3}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{1-b^{2}}\frac{vq}{1+v^{2}_{\star}q^{2}}\begin{pmatrix}1&-b\mathfrak{b}_{+}(q)\\
-b\mathfrak{b}_{-}(q)&1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}i+v_{\star}q&0\\
0&-i+v_{\star}q\end{pmatrix}

(2.51)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{1-b^{2}}\frac{v_{\star}q}{1+v^{2}_{\star}q^{2}}\begin{pmatrix}i+v_{\star}q&-b(i+v_{\star}q)\\
b(i-v_{\star}q)&-i+v_{\star}q\end{pmatrix}\;.

Therefore, inserting (
2.51
) into (
2.50
), the susceptibility matrix takes the form

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}^{0}(q)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi v_{\star}}\frac{v_{\star}q}{1+v^{2}_{\star}q^{2}}\frac{1}{1-b^{2}}\begin{pmatrix}1&-b\\
-b&1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}i+v_{\star}q&-b(i+v_{\star}q)\\
b(i-v_{\star}q)&-i+v_{\star}q\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi v_{\star}}\frac{v_{\star}q}{1+v^{2}_{\star}q^{2}}\frac{1}{1-b^{2}}\begin{pmatrix}(1-b^{2})i+(1+b^{2})vq&-2bv_{\star}q\\
-2bv_{\star}q&(b^{2}-1)i+(1+b^{2})v_{\star}q\end{pmatrix}\;;

finally, by summing over all the entries of
\mathcal{K}_{0}
we obtain the following simple form for the density response function:

\sum_{\omega,\omega^{\prime}=\pm}\mathcal{K}^{0}_{\omega\omega^{\prime}}(q)=\frac{1}{\pi v_{\star}}\frac{4\pi v_{\star}-\lambda_{\star}}{4\pi v_{\star}+\lambda_{\star}}\frac{v^{2}_{\star}q^{2}}{1+v^{2}_{\star}q^{2}}\;.

(2.52)

This expression for the susceptibility has been already derived rigorously, from a linear response ansatz, see
[
9
]
, Theorem 1.1, Eq. (1.15). To compute the current response, we proceed as follows. We write

\sum_{\omega,\omega^{\prime}=\pm}\mathcal{K}^{1}_{\omega\omega^{\prime}}(q)\equiv(\vec{1},\mathcal{K}^{1}(q)\vec{1})=v_{\star}\left(\vec{1},\mathcal{K}^{0}(q)\frac{1+b\sigma_{1}}{1-b\sigma_{1}}\sigma_{3}\vec{1}\right)

(2.53)

with
\vec{1}
the vector with all components equal to 1; now, we have the following identity:

\begin{split}\frac{1+b\sigma_{1}}{1-b\sigma_{1}}\sigma_{3}\vec{1}&=\sigma_{3}\frac{1-b\sigma_{1}}{1+b\sigma_{1}}\vec{1}=\sigma_{3}\frac{(1-b\sigma_{1})^{2}}{1-b^{2}}\vec{1}=\frac{(1-b)^{2}}{1-b^{2}}\sigma_{3}\vec{1}\;,\end{split}

where in the first step we used
\{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{3}\}=0
, and in the last one we exploited
\sigma_{1}\vec{1}=\vec{1}
.
This identity allows to write

(\vec{1},\mathcal{K}^{1}(q)\vec{1})=v_{\star}\frac{1-b}{1+b}(\vec{1},\mathcal{K}^{0}(q)\sigma_{3}\vec{1})

(2.54)

which, up to the prefactor, corresponds to summing the components of the first column of
\mathcal{K}^{0}
and subtracting the components of the second one. Therefore:

(\vec{1},\mathcal{K}^{1}(q)\vec{1})=\frac{1}{\pi}\frac{4\pi v_{\star}-\lambda_{\star}}{4\pi v_{\star}+\lambda_{\star}}\frac{iv_{\star}q}{1+v^{2}_{\star}q^{2}}\;.

(2.55)

Note that, owing to the oddness of the correlator (
2.55
),
\chi^{\mathrm{lin}}_{1}(x)\equiv 0
whenever
\mu_{\infty}
is even, as expected.

3
Duhamel expansion and Wick rotation

3.1
Auxiliary dynamics

Recall that
\eta_{\beta}
is the best approximation of
\eta
in
\frac{2\pi}{\beta}\mathbb{N}
, such that
\eta_{\beta}\geq\eta
. We define the auxiliary Hamiltonian by replacing the adiabatic parameter
\eta
with
\eta_{\beta}
:

\mathcal{H}_{\beta,\eta}(t)=\mathcal{H}+e^{\eta_{\beta}t}\theta\sum_{{\bf x}\in\Lambda_{L}}\mu(\theta x)a^{*}_{{\bf x}}a_{{\bf x}}\;.

(3.1)

Let us denote by
\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}(t;s)
the two-parameter unitary group generated by
\mathcal{H}_{\beta,\eta}(t)
,

i\partial_{t}\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}(t;s)=\mathcal{H}_{\beta,\eta}(t)\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}(t;s)\;,\qquad\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}(s;s)=\mathbbm{1}\;.

(3.2)

The next result allows to control the error introduced by replacing the dynamics generated by
\mathcal{H}(\eta t)
with the dynamics generated by
\mathcal{H}_{\beta,\eta}(t)
.

Proposition 3.1

(Approximation by the auxiliary dynamics)
.

Under the same assumptions of Theorem
2.6
, it follows that:

\Big\|\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}(t;-\infty)^{*}\mathcal{O}_{X}\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}(t;-\infty)-\mathcal{U}(t;-\infty)^{*}\mathcal{O}_{X}\mathcal{U}(t;-\infty)\Big\|\leq C\frac{\theta}{\eta^{3}\beta}

(3.3)

for some
C>0
independent of
\theta,\eta,\beta
.

Remark 3.2
.

Let us briefly comment on the reason for introducing the auxiliary dynamics. Let
\mathcal{A}
be such that
[\mathcal{A},\mathcal{N}]=0
, and let
\tilde{\tau}_{t}(\mathcal{A})=e^{\eta t}\tau_{t}(\mathcal{A})
, which we can view as a (non-unitary) regularization of the original dynamics, damped in the past. Then, if
\eta\in\frac{2\pi}{\beta}\mathbb{N}
, we observe that
\tilde{\tau}_{t}(\cdot)
satisfies the KMS identity:

\langle\tilde{\tau}_{t}(\mathcal{A})\mathcal{B}\rangle_{\beta,L}=\langle\mathcal{B}\tilde{\tau}_{t+i\beta}(\mathcal{A})\rangle_{\beta,L}\;,

(3.4)

where we used the trivial but crucial identity
e^{\eta t}=e^{\eta(t+i\beta)}
. The KMS identity for the regularized dynamics (
3.4
) plays a key role in the mapping of the real time Duhamel series into an imaginary time expansion, which can be efficiently studied
[
29
]
; see Proposition
2.5
above. Due to the fact that the difference between any
\eta>0
and its best approximation
\eta_{\beta}\in\frac{2\pi}{\beta}\mathbb{N}
vanishes as
\beta\to\infty
, this approach is suitable to study zero or low temperature systems (lower than some
\eta
-dependent value).

Proof of Proposition
3.1
.

The proof is a standard application of Lieb-Robinson bounds, we refer to Proposition 4.1 of
[
29
]
for the details.
∎

3.2
Duhamel expansion

Setting
\tilde{\rho}(t)=\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}(t;-\infty)\rho_{\beta,\mu,L}\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}(t;-\infty)^{*}
, by Proposition
3.1
we may write

\Tr j_{\nu,x}\rho(t)=\Tr j_{\nu,x}\widetilde{\rho}(t)+\mathcal{E}^{\beta,L}_{\nu}(x,t;\eta,\theta)\;,

(3.5)

with the error term
\mathcal{E}^{\beta,L}_{\nu}
satisfying the bound

|\mathcal{E}^{\beta,L}_{\nu}(x,t;\eta,\theta)|\leq C\frac{\theta}{\eta^{3}\beta}\;.

(3.6)

Consider the main term in the right-hand side of (
3.5
). By Proposition
2.5
:

\begin{split}&\Tr j_{\nu,x}\widetilde{\rho}(t)-\Tr j_{\nu,x}\rho_{\beta,\mu,L}\\
&\quad=\sum_{n\geq 1}\frac{(-1)^{n}e^{n\eta_{\beta}t}}{n!}\int_{[0,\beta)^{n}}d\underline{s}\,e^{-i\eta_{\beta}(s_{1}+\ldots+s_{n})}\langle{\bf T}\gamma_{s_{1}}(\mathcal{P});\cdots;\gamma_{s_{n}}(\mathcal{P});j_{\nu,x}\rangle_{\beta,L}\;.\end{split}

(3.7)

It is convenient to rewrite the
n
-th order contribution to the expansion in Fourier space. Recalling the definition (
2.37
) and the form of the perturbation (
2.22
),

\begin{split}&\int_{[0,\beta)^{n}}d\underline{s}\,e^{-i\eta_{\beta}(s_{1}+\ldots+s_{n})}\langle{\bf T}\gamma_{s_{1}}(\mathcal{P});\cdots;\gamma_{s_{n}}(\mathcal{P});j_{\nu,x}\rangle_{\beta,L}\\
&\quad=\int_{[0,\beta)^{n}}d\underline{s}\,e^{-i\eta_{\beta}(s_{1}+\ldots+s_{n})}\langle{\bf T}\gamma_{s_{1}}(j_{0}(\mu_{\theta}));\cdots;\gamma_{s_{n}}(j_{0}(\mu_{\theta}));j_{\nu,x}\rangle_{\beta,L}\;,\end{split}

(3.8)

using that

j_{0}(\mu_{\theta})=\frac{\theta}{L}\sum_{p\in B_{L}}\hat{\mu}_{\theta}(-p)\hat{\jmath}_{\nu,p}\;,\qquad\hat{\jmath}_{\nu,p}=\sum_{{\bf x}\in\Lambda_{L}}e^{-ipx}\hat{\jmath}_{\nu,{\bf x}}\;,

(3.9)

with
\hat{\mu}_{\theta}(p):=\hat{\mu}(p/\theta)/\theta
, and writing, for
\underline{p}=(p_{0},p_{1})
with
p_{1}=p
and
p_{0}=\eta_{\beta}
,

\hat{n}_{\underline{p}}:=\int_{0}^{\beta}ds\,e^{-i\eta_{\beta}s}\gamma_{s}(\hat{n}_{p})\;,

(3.10)

we can rewrite the right-hand side of (
3.8
) as:

\begin{split}&\int_{[0,\beta)^{n}}d\underline{s}\,e^{-i\eta_{\beta}(s_{1}+\ldots+s_{n})}\langle{\bf T}\gamma_{s_{1}}(j_{0}(\mu_{\theta}));\cdots;\gamma_{s_{n}}(j_{0}(\mu_{\theta}));j_{\nu,x}\rangle_{\beta,L}\\
&\qquad=\frac{\theta^{n}}{L^{n}}\sum_{\{p_{i}\}\in B_{L}^{n}}\Big[\prod_{i=1}^{n}\hat{\mu}_{\theta}(-p_{i})\Big]e^{ip_{n+1}x}\frac{1}{\beta L}\langle{\bf T}\,\hat{n}_{\underline{p}_{1}}\;;\cdots\;;\hat{n}_{\underline{p}_{n}}\;;\hat{\jmath}_{\nu,\underline{p}_{n+1}}\rangle_{\beta,L}\;,\end{split}

(3.11)

where
\underline{p}_{n+1}=-\underline{p}_{1}-\ldots-\underline{p}_{n}
. In order to derive (
3.11
), we used the space-time translation invariance of the Gibbs state.

Thus, we are led to the following representation for the Duhamel expansion of the response functions: recalling (
3.7
)-(
3.11
), we have

\begin{split}\chi^{\beta,L}_{\nu}(x;\eta,\theta)=-\sum_{m=2}^{\infty}&\frac{(-\theta)^{m-2}}{(m-1)!}\frac{1}{L^{m-1}}\sum_{\{p_{i}\}\in B_{L}^{m-1}}\Big[\prod_{j=1}^{m-1}\hat{\mu}_{\theta}(-p_{j})\Big]e^{ip_{m}x}\\
&\quad\cdot\frac{1}{\beta L}\langle\mathbf{T}\hat{n}_{\underline{p}_{1}};\cdots;\hat{n}_{\underline{p}_{m-1}};\hat{\jmath}_{\nu,\underline{p}_{m}}\rangle_{\beta,L}+E^{\beta,L}_{\nu}(x;\eta,\theta)\;,\end{split}

(3.12)

where:
\underline{p}_{i}:=(\eta_{\beta},p_{i})
and
\underline{p}_{m}:=-\sum_{i=1}^{m-1}\underline{p}_{i}
; the error term
E^{\beta,L}_{\nu}
satisfies, by (
3.6
):

|E^{\beta,L}_{\nu}(x;\eta,\theta)|=\frac{|\mathcal{E}^{\beta,L}_{\nu}(x,0;\eta,\theta)|}{\theta}\leq\frac{C}{\eta^{3}\beta}\;.

(3.13)

Starting from identity (
3.12
), the remaining part of the proof (and of the paper) will be devoted to the proof of suitable bounds for the Euclidean density-current correlations appearing in the higher order (
m\geq 3
) terms, and to the explicit evaluation of the linear (
m=2
) term.

Remark 3.3
.

Observe that we have written (
3.12
) in terms of the number of points of the correlation functions (
m
), instead of the Duhamel order (
n=m-1
).

4
RG analysis of the lattice model

4.1
Grassmann integral representation of the model

In this section, we will introduce a Grassmann integral formulation that will allow us to rewrite the density Euclidean
m
-point functions as derivatives of a suitable generating functional, that can be analyzed via multiscale methods.

Let
\chi:\mathbb{R}\to[0,1]
be a smooth, even cut-off function such that:

\chi(t)=\begin{cases}1&|t|\leq 1\\
0&|t|\geq\frac{3}{2}\end{cases}

(4.1)

and, for
N_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
set
\chi_{N_{0}}(\cdot)=\chi(2^{-N_{0}}\cdot)
. We define the set of space-time momenta
\mathbb{D}_{\beta,L,N_{0}}
as:

\mathbb{D}_{\beta,L,N_{0}}=\left\{\underline{k}=(k_{0},k)\in\frac{2\pi}{\beta}\Big(\mathbb{Z}+\frac{1}{2}\Big)\times B_{L}\ \Big|\ \chi_{N_{0}}(k_{0})>0\right\}\;.

(4.2)

Observe that the set
\mathbb{D}_{\beta,L,N_{0}}
is finite. Then, consider the Grassmann
\mathbb{C}
-algebra generated by the set of anti-commuting symbols
\{\hat{\Psi}^{\pm}_{\underline{k},\rho}\ |\ \underline{k}\in\mathbb{D}_{\beta,L,N_{0}},\rho\in S_{M}\}
. On such Grassmann algebra we define a Gaussian Grassmann measure
P_{N_{0}}[d\Psi]
as follows. First, we set the Berezin integral
d\Psi
to be the linear functional which is non-zero only on monomials of maximal degree, and such that its evaluation on such top degree monomials is:

\int d\Psi\prod_{\underline{k}\in\mathbb{D}_{\beta,L,N_{0}}}\prod_{\rho\in S_{M}}\hat{\Psi}^{-}_{\underline{k},\rho}\hat{\Psi}^{+}_{\underline{k},\rho}=1.

(4.3)

Let us define the free propagator as:

\hat{g}_{\rho\rho^{\prime}}^{(\leq N_{0})}(\underline{k})=\left(\frac{\chi_{N_{0}}(k_{0})}{ik_{0}+\hat{H}(k)-\mu}\right)_{\rho\rho^{\prime}}.

(4.4)

We define the associated Grassmann Gaussian integration
P_{N_{0}}[d\Psi]
as:

\int P_{N_{0}}[d\Psi](\cdot)=\frac{1}{\mathcal{Z}^{0}_{\beta,L,N_{0}}}\int d\Psi\exp\Big\{-\frac{1}{\beta L}\sum_{\underline{k}\in\mathbb{D}_{\beta,L,N_{0}}}(\hat{\Psi}^{+}_{\underline{k}},\hat{g}^{(\leq N_{0})}(\underline{k})^{-1}\hat{\Psi}^{-}_{\underline{k}})\Big\}\ \Big(\cdot\Big)

(4.5)

with the short-hand
(\phi,A\psi)=\sum_{\rho,\rho^{\prime}}\phi_{\rho}A_{\rho\rho^{\prime}}\psi_{\rho^{\prime}}
and normalization

\mathcal{Z}^{0}_{\beta,L,N_{0}}=\prod_{\underline{k}\in\mathbb{D}_{\beta,L,N_{0}}}[\beta L]\det[\hat{g}^{(\leq N_{0})}(\underline{k})].

(4.6)

Given this definition for
P_{N_{0}}[d\hat{\Psi}]
, it is easy to check that, for a given monomial
\prod_{i=1}^{n}\hat{\Psi}_{\underline{k}_{i},\rho_{i}}^{\epsilon_{i}}
its action yields zero if
|\{i:\epsilon_{i}=+\}|\neq|\{i:\epsilon_{i}=-\}|
; otherwise,

\int P_{N_{0}}[d\Psi]\prod_{i=1}^{m}\hat{\Psi}^{-}_{\underline{k}_{i},\rho_{i}}\hat{\Psi}^{+}_{\underline{p}_{i},\rho^{\prime}_{i}}=\det[K(\underline{k}_{i},\rho_{i};\underline{p}_{j},\rho^{\prime}_{j})]_{i,j=1}^{m}

(4.7)

with
K(\underline{k},\rho;\underline{p},\rho^{\prime})=\beta L\delta_{\underline{k},\underline{p}}\,\hat{g}_{\rho\rho^{\prime}}^{(\leq N_{0})}(\underline{k})
. Eq. (
4.7
) is the Wick’s rule, for Grassmann Gaussian fields.

Next, let us introduce the real-space fields as:

\Psi^{\pm}_{\underline{x},\rho}=\frac{1}{\beta L}\sum_{\underline{k}\in\mathbb{D}_{\beta,L,N_{0}}}e^{\mp i\underline{k}\cdot(\underline{x}-\underline{y})}\hat{\Psi}^{\pm}_{\underline{k},\rho}\;.

(4.8)

One can check that:

\int P_{N_{0}}[d\Psi]\Psi^{-}_{\underline{x},\rho}\Psi^{+}_{\underline{y},\rho^{\prime}}=g_{\rho\rho^{\prime}}^{(\leq N_{0})}(\underline{x};\underline{y}),

(4.9)

where the real-space propagator is:

g_{\rho\rho^{\prime}}^{(\leq N_{0})}(\underline{x};\underline{y})=\sum_{\underline{k}\in\mathbb{D}_{\beta,L,N_{0}}}e^{i\underline{k}\cdot(\underline{x}-\underline{y})}\hat{g}_{\rho\rho^{\prime}}^{(\leq N_{0})}(\underline{k}).

(4.10)

It is well-known that, as
\underline{x}\neq\underline{y}
, the
N_{0}\to\infty
limit of the real-space two-point function converges to the Euclidean two-point function of the non-interacting model,

\lim_{N_{0}\to\infty}g_{\rho\rho^{\prime}}^{(\leq N_{0})}(\underline{x};\underline{y})=\langle{\bf T}\gamma_{x_{0}}(a_{x,\rho})\gamma_{y_{0}}(a^{*}_{y,\rho^{\prime}})\rangle_{\beta,L}^{0}\equiv g_{\rho\rho^{\prime}}(\underline{x};\underline{y})\;.

(4.11)

Let us now define the interacting Grassmann field theory. The Grassmann interaction is:

V(\Psi):=\lambda\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}_{\beta}}dx_{0}dy_{0}\sum_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\Lambda_{L}}n_{\underline{\mathbf{x}}}\delta(x_{0}-y_{0})w(x,y)n_{\underline{\mathbf{y}}}

(4.12)

with
\underline{\mathbf{x}}=(\underline{x},\rho)
and with
n_{\underline{\mathbf{x}}}=\Psi^{+}_{\underline{\mathbf{x}}}\Psi^{-}_{\underline{\mathbf{x}}}
. It should be noted that the factors
-1/2
are absent in this Grassmann representation; this is related to the fact that:

\lim_{N_{0}\to\infty}g_{\rho\rho^{\prime}}^{(\leq N_{0})}((x_{0},x);(x_{0},y))=g_{\rho\rho^{\prime}}((x_{0},x);(x_{0},y))-\frac{\delta_{{\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}}}}{2}\;.

(4.13)

We can rewrite expression (
4.12
) in momentum space as:

V(\Psi):=\frac{1}{\beta L}\sum_{\underline{p}\in\mathbb{M}_{\beta,L,N_{0}}}\lambda\hat{w}(p)\,\hat{n}_{-\underline{p}}\hat{n}_{\underline{p}}\;,

(4.14)

where
\mathbb{M}_{\beta,L,N_{0}}:=\{\underline{p}=(p_{0},p)\in\frac{2\pi}{\beta}\mathbb{Z}\times B_{L}\ |\ \chi_{N_{0}}(p_{0})>0\}
and:

\hat{n}_{\underline{p}}=\int_{0}^{\beta}dx_{0}\sum_{\mathbf{x}\in\Lambda_{L}}e^{-i\underline{p}\cdot\underline{x}}\,n_{\underline{\mathbf{x}}}=\frac{1}{\beta L}\sum_{\underline{k}\in\mathbb{D}_{\beta,L,N_{0}}}(\hat{\Psi}^{+}_{\underline{k}-\underline{p}},\hat{J}_{\nu}(k,p)\hat{\Psi}^{-}_{\underline{k}})\;.

(4.15)

At last, we introduce the partition function of this Grassmann model as:

\mathcal{Z}_{\beta,L,N_{0}}:=\int P_{N_{0}}[d\Psi]\,e^{-V(\Psi)}\;.

(4.16)

We are now ready to define the generating functional of the Grassmann field theory. Let us start by introducing the source terms. Using the short-hand notation:

\int_{\beta,L}d\underline{x}:=\int_{\mathbb{T}_{\beta}}dx_{0}\sum_{x\in\Gamma_{L}},

(4.17)

and considering two Grassmann fields
\phi^{\pm}_{\underline{x},\rho}
and a real valued vector field
A^{\nu}_{\underline{x}}
, we set

B(\Psi;\phi):=\int_{\beta,L}d\underline{x}\,(\phi^{+}_{\underline{x}},\Psi^{-}_{\underline{x}})+(\Psi^{+}_{\underline{x}},\phi^{-}_{\underline{x}})\;,\qquad C(\Psi;A):=\sum_{\nu=0,1}\int_{\beta,L}d\underline{x}\,A^{\nu}_{\underline{x}}\,j_{\nu,\underline{x}}\;,

(4.18)

with
j_{\nu,\underline{x}}
the Grassmann counterpart of the lattice 2-current defined in (
2.34
):

j_{\nu,x}=\frac{1}{\beta L}\sum_{\underline{p}\in\mathbb{M}_{\beta,L,N}}e^{i\underline{p}\cdot\underline{x}}\hat{\jmath}_{\nu,\underline{p}}\qquad\hat{\jmath}_{\nu,\underline{p}}=\frac{1}{\beta L}\sum_{\underline{k}\in\mathbb{D}_{\beta,L,N}}(\hat{\Psi}^{+}_{\underline{k}-\underline{p}},\hat{J}_{\nu}(k,p)\hat{\Psi}^{-}_{\underline{k}})\;.

(4.19)

In order to make sure that the field
\phi^{\pm}
also generates a finite dimensional algebra, we will suppose that it is supported for finitely many Fourier modes.

The generating functional of the correlation functions
\mathcal{W}_{\beta,L,N_{0}}(\phi,A)
is then given by:

\mathcal{W}_{\beta,L,N_{0}}(\phi,A)=\log\int P_{N_{0}}[d\Psi]e^{-V(\Psi)+B(\Psi;\phi)+C(\Psi;A)}\;.

(4.20)

A corollary of the construction that we are going to describe is that, for
\|A\|_{\infty},|\lambda|
small enough, the argument of the logarithm is non-zero, and hence the generating functional is well defined at finite
\beta,L,N_{0}
.

It is well-known that correlation functions of the Gibbs state of the model in Fock space at non-coinciding space-time points can be represented as functional derivatives of the generating functional (
4.20
), in the limit
N_{0}\to\infty
; see
e.g.

[
26
]
for a discussion on this point. In particular, we are interested in the following type of momentum-space correlation functions:

\langle\mathbf{T}\hat{n}_{\underline{p}_{1}};\,\cdots;\hat{n}_{\underline{p}_{m-1}};\hat{\jmath}_{\nu,\underline{p}_{m}}\rangle_{\beta,L}=\lim_{N_{0}\to\infty}(\beta L)^{m}\left.\frac{\partial^{m}\mathcal{W}_{\beta,L,N_{0}}(\phi,A)}{\partial\hat{A}^{0}_{-\underline{p}_{1}}\cdots\,\partial\hat{A}^{0}_{-\underline{p}_{m-1}}\partial\hat{A}^{\nu}_{-\underline{p}_{m}}}\right|_{A=\phi=0}\;.

(4.21)

The identity (
4.21
) will allow us to use the properties of Grassmann Gaussian fields in order to investigate the Euclidean correlation functions of our model. Equation (
4.21
) holds in the analyticity domain of the Grassmann theory, which is contained in the analyticity domain of the Fock space model.

4.2
Integration of the ultraviolet degrees of freedom

As in all applications of renormalization group to condensed matter problems, the starting point is the integration of the ultraviolet degrees of freedom. After this step, which will be briefly described in this Section (referring to other works for the technical details), the problem will be reduced to the analysis of a suitable, effective infrared model.

Let
\chi(\cdot)
be a smooth cutoff function as in (
4.1
). We decompose
\hat{g}^{(\leq N_{0})}
into the sum of an ultraviolet (UV) and infrared (IR) part as:

\hat{g}^{(\leq N_{0})}(\underline{k})=\hat{g}^{\text{UV}}(\underline{k})+\hat{g}^{\text{IR}}(\underline{k})\;,\qquad\hat{g}^{\text{UV}}(\underline{k})=\frac{\chi_{[0,N_{0}]}(k_{0})}{ik_{0}+\hat{H}(k)-\mu}

(4.22)

where
\chi_{[0,N_{0}]}(k_{0})=\chi_{N_{0}}(k_{0})-\chi(k_{0})
. By the addition principle of Grassmann Gaussian variables, we split the Grassmann field as:

\hat{\Psi}^{\pm}_{\underline{k}}=\hat{\Psi}^{\text{UV},\pm}_{\underline{k}}+\hat{\Psi}^{\text{IR}}_{\underline{k}}\;,

(4.23)

where
\hat{\Psi}^{\text{UV},\pm}_{\underline{k}}
,
\hat{\Psi}^{\text{IR}}_{\underline{k}}
are independent Grassmann fields, with propagators give by, respectively,
\hat{g}^{\text{UV}}(\underline{k})
and
\hat{g}^{\text{IR}}(\underline{k})
. Thanks to the fact that the cutoff function
\chi_{[0,N_{0}]}(k_{0})
is vanishing for
|k_{0}|\leq 1
, the propagator
\hat{g}^{\text{UV}}(\underline{k})
is non-singular, uniformly in
\beta,L
. Let:

V(\Psi;A,\phi)=-V(\Psi)+B(\Psi;\phi)+C(\Psi;A)\;,

(4.24)

recall (
4.20
). We then rewrite the generating functional of correlations as:

e^{\mathcal{W}_{\beta,L,N_{0}}(\phi,A)}=\int P_{\text{IR}}[d\Psi^{\text{IR}}]\int P_{\text{UV}}[d\Psi^{\text{UV}}]e^{V(\Psi^{\text{IR}}+\Psi^{\text{UV}};A,\phi)}\;,

(4.25)

and we proceed with the integration of the ultraviolet field. A standard multiscale analysis for the ultraviolet degrees of freedom yields, see
e.g.

[
26
]
:

\int P_{\text{UV}}[d\Psi^{\text{UV}}]e^{V(\Psi^{\text{IR}}+\Psi^{\text{UV}};A,\phi)}=e^{\mathcal{W}^{\text{IR}}_{\beta,L,N_{0}}(\phi,A)+V^{\text{IR}}(\Psi^{\text{IR}};\phi,A)}\;,

(4.26)

where the argument of the exponential have the form:

V^{\text{IR}}(\Psi^{\text{IR}};\phi,A)=\sum_{\Gamma=(\Gamma_{\Psi},\Gamma_{\phi},\Gamma_{A})}\int_{\beta,L}d\underline{X}d\underline{Y}d\underline{Z}\,\Psi^{\text{IR}}_{\Gamma_{\Psi}}(\underline{X})\phi_{\Gamma_{\phi}}(\underline{Y})A_{\Gamma_{A}}(\underline{Z})W^{\text{IR}}_{\Gamma}(\underline{X},\underline{Y},\underline{Z})\;,

(4.27)

and:

\mathcal{W}^{\text{IR}}_{\beta,L,N_{0}}(\phi,A)=\sum_{\Gamma=(\Gamma_{\phi},\Gamma_{A})}\int_{\beta,L}d\underline{Y}d\underline{Z}\,\phi_{\Gamma_{\phi}}(\underline{Y})A_{\Gamma_{A}}(\underline{Z})W^{\text{IR}}_{\Gamma}(\underline{Y},\underline{Z})\;;

(4.28)

let us explain the meaning of the various objects entering these expressions.

1.

The labels
\Gamma_{\Psi},\Gamma_{\phi},\Gamma_{A}
denote the collection of all labels of the fields, integrated and external. That is, if
n
is the order of the
\Psi
-monomial,
\Gamma_{\Psi}=\{(\rho_{1},\varepsilon_{1}),\ldots,(\rho_{n},\varepsilon_{n})\}
, and:

\Psi_{\Gamma_{\Psi}}(\underline{X})=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\Psi^{\varepsilon_{i}}_{\underline{x}_{i},\rho_{i}}

(4.29)

with the understanding that
\underline{X}=(\underline{x}_{1},\ldots,\underline{x}_{n})
. If
r
is the order of the
\phi
-monomial,
\Gamma_{\phi}=\{(\rho_{1},\varepsilon_{1}),\ldots,(\rho_{r},\varepsilon_{r})\}
, and:

\Phi_{\Gamma_{\Phi}}(\underline{Y})=\prod_{i=1}^{r}\phi^{\varepsilon_{i}}_{\underline{y}_{i},\rho_{i}}\;,

(4.30)

with
\underline{Y}=(\underline{y}_{1},\ldots,\underline{y}_{r})
. If
m
is the order of the
A
-monomial,
\Gamma_{A}=(\nu_{1},\ldots,\nu_{m})
, and:

A_{\Gamma_{A}}(\underline{Z})=\prod_{i=1}^{m}A^{\nu_{i}}_{\underline{z}_{i}}\;,

(4.31)

with
\underline{Z}=(\underline{z}_{1},\ldots,\underline{z}_{m})
.

2.

The integral runs over all the space-time labels of the monomials determined by
\Gamma
.

3.

The functions
W^{\text{IR}}_{\Gamma}(\underline{X},\underline{Y},\underline{Z})
, with the understanding that
W^{\text{IR}}_{(\emptyset,\Gamma_{\phi},\Gamma_{A})}(\underline{X},\underline{Y},\underline{Z})\equiv W^{\text{IR}}_{(\Gamma_{\phi},\Gamma_{A})}(\underline{Y},\underline{Z})
, also called kernels, are the outcome of the integration of the ultraviolet field. They are analytic in
\lambda
for
|\lambda|
small enough, they are invariant under simultaneous equal translation of all space-time coordinates, and they satisfy the following bound:

\int_{\beta,L}D\underline{Q}\prod_{i<j}\|\underline{q}_{i}-\underline{q}_{j}\|_{\beta,L}^{n_{ij}}|W^{\text{IR}}_{\Gamma}(\underline{Q})|\leq\beta LC_{\Gamma;\{n_{ij}\}}\quad\forall n_{ij}\in\mathbb{N},

(4.32)

with
\underline{Q}=(\underline{X},\underline{Y},\underline{Z})=(\underline{q}_{1},\ldots,\underline{q}_{n+m+r})
, where
\|\cdot\|_{\beta,L}
denotes the periodic space-time norm:

\|\underline{q}\|_{\beta,L}=\inf_{(n_{0},n_{1})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\|\underline{q}+(n_{0}\beta,n_{1}L)\|\;.

(4.33)

The kernels
W^{\text{IR}}_{\Gamma}(\underline{Q})
play the role of new effective interactions for the infrared field. The analyticity of the effective interactions is a nontrivial result, and it follows from the application of the Brydges-Battle-Federbush (BBF) formula, for fermionic truncated expectations. This formula provides a convergent representation of fermionic perturbation theory; see
e.g.

[
22
,
27
]
for a review. Observe that the expressions (
4.27
), (
4.28
) actually involve finitely many monomials
\Psi^{\text{IR}}
and
\phi
, due to the fact that these Grassmann fields involve finitely many Fourier modes, and hence generate a finite-dimensional Grassmann algebra. Instead, the sum over the
A
monomials can be proved to be convergent, for
\|A\|_{\infty}
small enough. Thus, both (
4.27
), (
4.28
) are well defined.

We are left with the integration of the field
\Psi^{\text{IR}\pm}
. Its propagator is:

\hat{g}^{\text{IR}}(\underline{k})=\frac{\chi(k_{0})}{ik_{0}+\hat{H}(k)-\mu}\;,

(4.34)

which is now singular as
\beta,L\to\infty
, for
k_{0}=0
and for
k
equal to one of the Fermi points
k_{F}^{\omega}
. Let
k_{F}^{\omega}(\lambda)
be points in
B_{L}
such that
|k_{F}^{\omega}-k_{F}^{\omega}(\lambda)|\leq C|\lambda|
, to be fixed later on; they play the role of renormalized Fermi points. It is convenient to rewrite the infrared propagator as:

\hat{g}^{\text{IR}}(\underline{k})=\hat{g}^{\text{IR},1}(\underline{k})+\hat{g}^{\text{IR},2}(\underline{k})

(4.35)

where, for
\underline{k}_{F}^{\omega}(\lambda)=(0,k_{F}^{\omega}(\lambda))
:

\hat{g}^{\text{IR},1}(\underline{k})=\sum_{\omega=1}^{N_{f}}\frac{\chi_{0}(\|\underline{k}-\underline{k}_{F}^{\omega}(\lambda)\|_{\omega})}{ik_{0}+\hat{H}(k)-\mu}\;,

(4.36)

with:
\chi_{0}(\cdot)=\chi(\delta(\cdot))
for
\delta>0
small enough;
\|\underline{q}\|_{\omega}^{2}=|q_{0}|^{2}+v_{\omega}^{2}|q_{1}|_{\mathbb{T}}^{2}
; and
\hat{g}^{\text{IR},2}(\underline{k})
is a smooth, bounded function of
\underline{k}=(k_{0},k_{1})
. The number
\delta
is chosen so that the cutoffs
\chi_{0}(\|\underline{k}-\underline{k}_{F}^{\omega}(\lambda)\|_{\omega})
have disjoint supports, and so that
\chi_{0}(\|\underline{k}-\underline{k}_{F}^{\omega}(\lambda)\|_{\omega})\neq 0
implies
\chi(k_{0})=1
. The advantage of the rewriting (
4.35
) is that the cutoffs entering the massless propagator
\hat{g}^{\text{IR},1}(\underline{k})
are symmetric in
k_{0}
and in
v_{\omega}[k_{1}-k_{F}^{\omega}(\lambda)]
.

We use the addition principle to write:

\hat{\Psi}^{\text{IR},\pm}_{\underline{k}}=\hat{\Psi}^{\text{IR}1,\pm}_{\underline{k}}+\hat{\Psi}^{\text{IR}2,\pm}_{\underline{k}}\;,

(4.37)

and to integrate the field
\hat{\Psi}^{\text{IR}2,\pm}_{\underline{k}}
via fermionic cluster expansion, thus producing a new effective action for the field
\hat{\Psi}^{\text{IR}1,\pm}_{\underline{k}}
, determined by kernels
W^{\text{IR}1}_{\Gamma}(\cdot)
satisfying the same bounds as
W^{\text{IR}}_{\Gamma}(\cdot)
, with different numerical constants.

Let us now focus on the massless propagator
\hat{g}^{\text{IR},1}(\underline{k})
. Thanks to Assumption
2.1
, we know that the spectrum in a window of size
\Delta
around
\mu
is characterised by the local band functions
\{e_{\omega}:I_{\omega}\to\mathbb{R}\}_{\omega=1,\dots,N_{f}}
, so that the IR part may be rewritten to be

\hat{g}^{\text{IR}1}(\underline{k})=\sum_{\omega=1}^{N_{f}}\hat{g}^{\text{IR}}_{\omega}(\underline{k})P_{\omega}(k)\;,

(4.38)

with
P_{\omega}(k)
the rank-one projector onto the eigenvector associated with the eigenvalue
e_{\omega}(k)
, namely

P_{\omega}(k):=|\xi^{\omega}(k)\rangle\langle\xi^{\omega}(k)|\qquad\qquad\hat{H}(k)\xi^{\omega}(k)=e_{\omega}(k)\xi^{\omega}(k)\;,

(4.39)

and where the propagators
\hat{g}^{\text{IR}}_{\omega}(\underline{k})
are given by:

\hat{g}^{\text{IR}}_{\omega}(\underline{k})=\frac{\chi_{0}(\|\underline{k}-\underline{k}_{F}^{\omega}(\lambda)\|_{\omega})}{ik_{0}+e_{\omega}(k)-\mu}\;.

(4.40)

By the addition principle, we can represent the field
\hat{\Psi}^{\text{IR}1,\pm}_{\underline{k}}
as:

\hat{\Psi}^{\text{IR}1,\pm}_{\underline{k},\rho}=\sum_{\omega=1}^{N_{f}}\hat{\Psi}^{\text{IR}1,\pm}_{\underline{k},\omega,\rho}

(4.41)

where the field
\hat{\Psi}^{\text{IR}1,\pm}_{\underline{k},\omega,\rho}
has propagator given by
\hat{g}^{\text{IR}}_{\omega}(\underline{k})P_{\omega,\rho,\rho^{\prime}}(k)
. This Gaussian Grassmann field can be conveniently represented as, for all
\underline{k}
in the support of
\chi_{0}(\|\underline{k}-\underline{k}_{F}^{\omega}\|_{\omega})
:

\hat{\Psi}^{\text{IR}1,-}_{\underline{k},\omega,\rho}=\psi^{\text{IR},-}_{\underline{k},\omega}\xi^{\omega}_{\rho}(k)\;,

(4.42)

where the new Grassmann field
\psi^{\text{IR},\pm}_{\underline{k},\omega}
has propagator given by
\hat{g}^{\text{IR}}_{\omega}(\underline{k})
in (
4.40
). The fields
\{\psi^{\text{IR},\pm}_{\underline{k},\omega}\}
are quasi-particle fields, and they characterize the low-energy excitations of the system.

Let us now represent the effective action in terms of these new fields. Let:

\check{\xi}^{\omega}_{\rho}(x)=\frac{1}{L}\sum_{k\in B_{L}}e^{ikx}\chi\big(|k-k_{F}^{\omega}(\lambda)|_{\mathbb{T}}/2\big)\hat{\xi}_{\rho}^{\omega}(k)\;.

(4.43)

Observe that, from (
4.41
), (
4.42
):

\Psi^{\text{IR}1,-}_{\underline{x},\rho}=\sum_{\omega}(\check{\xi}^{\omega}_{\rho}*\psi^{\text{IR},-}_{\omega})_{\underline{x}}\;,\quad\Psi^{\text{IR}1,+}_{\underline{x},\rho}=\sum_{\omega}(\overline{\check{\xi}^{\omega}_{\rho}}*\psi^{\text{IR},+}_{\omega})_{\underline{x}}

(4.44)

with
*
denoting spatial convolution:

(\check{\xi}^{\omega}_{\rho}*\psi^{\text{IR},-}_{\omega})_{(x_{0},x)}=\sum_{z\in\Gamma_{L}}\check{\xi}^{\omega}_{\rho}(x-z)\psi^{\text{IR},-}_{(x_{0},z),\omega}\;.

(4.45)

Since the field
\psi^{\text{IR},\pm}_{\underline{k},\omega}
has the same support properties of
\hat{g}^{\text{IR}}_{\omega}(\underline{k})
, in Eq. (
4.42
) we can freely multiply the Bloch function
\xi^{\omega}_{\rho}(k)
by a cutoff function which is equal to
1
in the support of the field; this yields (
4.44
) with
\check{\xi}^{\omega}_{\rho}
as in (
4.45
). A standard integration by parts argument, combined with the smoothness of the cutoff function and of
\hat{\xi}^{\omega}(k)
around the
\omega
-Fermi point, shows that:

|\check{\xi}_{\rho}(x)|\leq\frac{C_{n}}{1+|x|_{L}^{n}}\qquad\text{for all $n\in\mathbb{N}$.}

(4.46)

Thus, we obtained the following rewriting of the generating functional of the correlations:

\begin{split}e^{\mathcal{W}_{\beta,L,N_{0}}(\phi,A)}&=e^{\mathcal{W}^{\text{IR}1}_{\beta,L,N_{0}}(\phi,A)}\int P_{\text{IR}1}[d\Psi^{\text{IR}1}]e^{V^{\text{IR}1}(\Psi^{\text{IR}1};\phi,A)}\\
&=e^{\mathcal{W}^{\text{IR}1}_{\beta,L,N_{0}}(\phi,A)}\int P_{\text{IR}}[d\psi^{\text{IR}}]e^{V^{\text{IR}1}(\psi^{\text{IR}}*\xi;\phi,A)}\;,\end{split}

(4.47)

where in the last step we used the representation (
4.44
) of the field
\Psi^{\text{IR}1}
. Let
\{\nu_{\omega}\}
be real numbers, such that
|\nu_{\omega}|\leq C|\lambda|
, to be determined later on. We further add and subtract a quadratic term in the effective interaction, up to a redefinition of the Gaussian integration:

\begin{split}&e^{\mathcal{W}^{\text{IR}1}_{\beta,L,N_{0}}(\phi,A)}\int P_{\text{IR}}[d\psi^{\text{IR}}]e^{V^{\text{IR}1}(\psi^{\text{IR}}*\xi;\phi,A)}\\
&=e^{\mathcal{W}^{\text{IR}1}_{\beta,L,N_{0}}(\phi,A)}\int P_{\text{IR}}[d\psi^{\text{IR}}]e^{\sum_{\omega}\nu_{\omega}\int_{\beta,L}d\underline{x}\,\psi^{\text{IR},+}_{\underline{x},\omega}\psi^{\text{IR},-}_{\underline{x},\omega}}e^{V^{\text{IR}1}(\psi^{\text{IR}}*\xi;\phi,A)-\sum_{\omega}\nu_{\omega}\int_{\beta,L}d\underline{x}\,\psi^{\text{IR},+}_{\underline{x},\omega}\psi^{\text{IR},-}_{\underline{x},\omega}}\\
&\equiv e^{\mathcal{W}^{(0)}_{\beta,L,N_{0}}(\phi,A)}\int P_{0}[d\psi^{(\leq 0)}]e^{V^{(0)}(\psi^{(\leq 0)};\phi,A)}\;,\end{split}

(4.48)

where, setting
\underline{k}^{\prime}=\underline{k}-\underline{k}_{F}^{\omega}(\lambda)
, the new field
\psi^{(\leq 0)\pm}_{\underline{k}^{\prime},\omega}
has propagator given by:

\hat{g}_{\omega}^{(\leq 0)}(\underline{k}^{\prime})=\frac{\chi_{0}(\|\underline{k}^{\prime}\|_{\omega})}{ik_{0}+e_{\omega}(k^{\prime}+k_{F}^{\omega}(\lambda))-\mu+\nu_{\omega}\chi_{0}(\|\underline{k}^{\prime}\|_{\omega})}\;.

(4.49)

The variable
\underline{k}^{\prime}
expresses the relative shift of the Euclidean quasi-momentum with respect to the Fermi points. Thus, the configuration-space counterpart of the new field is:

e^{\mp i\underline{k}^{\omega}_{F}(\lambda)\cdot\underline{x}}\psi^{(\leq 0)\pm}_{\underline{x},\omega}\qquad\text{with}\quad\psi^{(\leq 0)\pm}_{\underline{x},\omega}=\frac{1}{\beta L}\sum_{\underline{k}^{\prime}}e^{\mp i\underline{k}^{\prime}\cdot\underline{x}}\psi^{(\leq 0)\pm}_{\underline{k}^{\prime},\omega}\;.

(4.50)

From (
4.49
), observe that the new Gaussian integration takes into account the first exponential factor in (
4.48
). The change of normalization of the Grassmann integration is taken into account by
\mathcal{W}^{(\leq 0)}_{\beta,L,N_{0}}(\phi,A)
, while the new effective interaction
V^{(0)}(\psi^{(\leq 0)};\phi,A)
takes into account all the remaining contributions appearing at the exponent. It has the form:

V^{(0)}(\psi^{(\leq 0)};\phi,A)=\sum_{\Gamma=(\Gamma_{\psi},\Gamma_{\phi},\Gamma_{A})}\int_{\beta,L}d\underline{X}d\underline{Y}d\underline{Z}\,\psi^{(\leq 0)}_{\Gamma_{\psi}}(\underline{X})\phi_{\Gamma_{\phi}}(\underline{Y})A_{\Gamma_{A}}(\underline{Z})W^{(0)}_{\Gamma}(\underline{X},\underline{Y},\underline{Z})\;,

(4.51)

where now
\Gamma_{\psi}=\{(\omega_{1},\varepsilon_{1}),\ldots,(\omega_{n},\varepsilon_{n})\}
.

Thanks to the decay properties of
\check{\xi}
, and to the properties of the kernels of the field
\Psi^{\text{IR}1}
, the new kernels are: analytic in
\lambda
for
|\lambda|
small enough; invariant under translation of all space-time arguments; they satisfy the decay estimate:

\int_{\beta,L}D\underline{Q}\prod_{i<j}\|\underline{q}_{i}-\underline{q}_{j}\|_{\beta,L}^{n_{ij}}|W^{(0)}_{\Gamma}(\underline{Q})|\leq\beta L\widetilde{C}_{\Gamma;\{n_{ij}\}}\quad\forall n_{ij}\in\mathbb{N},

(4.52)

which is the analogue of (
4.32
), with
C_{\{n_{ij}\}}<\widetilde{C}_{\{n_{ij}\}}\leq 2C_{\{n_{ij}\}}
for
|\lambda|
small enough. Observe that, if
\Gamma_{\psi}\neq\emptyset
, the bound is proportional to
|\lambda|
; actually, it is
|\lambda|^{|\Gamma_{\psi}|/4}
if
|\Gamma_{\psi}|\geq 4
. All the bounds are uniform in the ultraviolet cutoff
N_{0}
; from now on, we will not carry on the dependence on
N_{0}
(the limit
N_{0}\to\infty
will be eventually taken at the end).

Finally, let us comment on the necessity for “adding and subtracting” the quadratic terms proportional to
\nu_{\omega}
. These quadratic terms will play the role of counterterms for the renormalization group iteration, discussed in the next Sections. In particular, they will be chosen to control the flow of the relevant terms, which are the quadratic terms in the fermionic fields. The shifted Fermi points
k^{\omega}_{F}(\lambda)
will be chosen in order to take into account the shift of the singularity of (
4.49
), due to the presence of
\nu_{\omega}
as the denominator.

More precisely, the pair
(k_{F}^{\omega}(\lambda),\nu_{\omega})
will satisfy the condition, as
\beta,L\to\infty
:

e_{\omega}(k_{F}^{\omega}(\lambda))-\mu+\nu_{\omega}=0\;,

(4.53)

and with
|k_{F}^{\omega}(\lambda)-k_{F}^{\omega}(0)|\leq C|\lambda|
,
|\nu_{\omega}|\leq C|\lambda|
. For
L
finite, equation (
4.53
) may not have a solution, due to the condition
k_{F}^{\omega}(\lambda)\in B_{L}
. To accommodate this, we will instead require that the condition (
4.53
) holds with
0
on the right-hand side replaced by
O(1/L)
.

To conclude, we obtained the following rewriting for the generating functional of the correlations:

\mathcal{W}_{\beta,L}(\phi,A)=\mathcal{W}^{(0)}_{\beta,L}(\phi,A)+\log\int P_{0}[d\psi^{(\leq 0)}]e^{V^{(0)}(\psi^{(\leq 0)};\phi,A)}\;,

(4.54)

where
\mathcal{W}^{(0)}
,
V^{(0)}
have good analyticity and locality properties (kernels that decay faster than any power in Euclidean space). Renormalization group allows to further integrate the massless field
\psi^{(\leq 0)}
, via a renormalized, convergent expansion. This will be the content of the next section.

4.3
Renormalization group analysis

In this section we discuss the multiscale integration of the massless fields
\psi^{(\leq 0)}_{\omega}
. This is done via a renormalization group strategy, that has been carried out in a number of earlier references; see
e.g.

[
1
,
35
]
